Theorem 3.2

([26, Theorem 11.2]) With the above notation, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsf{WF}(\mu )=d_S^\vee (\tau {\widetilde{u}}_0^\vee )$$\end{document}WF(μ)=dS∨(τu~0∨). If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu =\sigma $$\end{document}μ=σ is cuspidal, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsf{WF}(\sigma )=d_S^\vee (\tau u_0^\vee )$$\end{document}WF(σ)=dS∨(τu0∨).

This means that the canonical unramified wavefront set of a supercuspidal representation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi (x,{{\widetilde{\sigma }}})$$\end{document}π(x,σ~) is3.1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {}^K\!{\underline{\textsf{WF}}}(\pi (x,{{\widetilde{\sigma }}}))=(d_S^\vee (\tau u_0^\vee ),x). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}KWF_(π(x,σ~))=(dS∨(τu0∨),x).Since we are in a depth-zero case, the Langlands parameter \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi $$\end{document}φ is trivial on the wild inertia group, and the image \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi (I_{{\textsf{k}}})$$\end{document}φ(Ik) is a cyclic group generated by an element \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda \in {}^L\!G$$\end{document}λ∈LG of finite order prime to q. Hence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\mathfrak {h}}}^\vee ={{\mathfrak {g}}^\vee }^\lambda $$\end{document}h∨=g∨λ is a pseudo-Levi subalgebra of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathfrak {g}}^\vee $$\end{document}g∨. Conjecture 1.6 is equivalent to3.2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} G\cdot d_S^\vee (\tau u_0^\vee )\le d^\vee (G^\vee \cdot {\mathcal {O}}_{\lambda ,s}^\vee ). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}G·dS∨(τu0∨)≤d∨(G∨·Oλ,s∨).We do not know a uniform way to attach the Langlands parameter to a depth-zero supercuspidal representation, so we will only verify the conjecture in a number of cases that exist in the literature.

Regular supercuspidal representations

For all regular depth-zero supercuspidal representations [11, 22], the conjecture is trivially true since in that case \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\mathfrak {h}}}^\vee $$\end{document}h∨ is just a Cartan subalgebra of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathfrak {g}}^\vee $$\end{document}g∨, so the only possible Langlands nilpotent element is 0. Hence the conjecture just says that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${}^{{\bar{{{\textsf{k}}}}}}\textsf{WF}(\pi )\le {\mathcal {O}}_p$$\end{document}k¯WF(π)≤Op, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {O}}_p$$\end{document}Op is the principal \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\textbf{G}}}({\bar{{{\textsf{k}}}}})$$\end{document}G(k¯)-orbit, which is true, but trivial. On the other hand, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${}^{{\bar{{{\textsf{k}}}}}}\textsf{WF}(\pi )={\mathcal {O}}_p$$\end{document}k¯WF(π)=Op if and only if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi $$\end{document}π is a generic representation, and this is not always the case. In general, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${}^{{\bar{{{\textsf{k}}}}}}\textsf{WF}(\pi )$$\end{document}k¯WF(π) can be equal to the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\textbf{G}}}({\bar{{{\textsf{k}}}}})$$\end{document}G(k¯)-saturation of the principal nilpotent orbit in a maximal pseudo-Levi, as we explain next.

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I_t=I_{{\textsf{k}}}/I^+_{{\textsf{k}}}$$\end{document}It=Ik/Ik+ be the tame quotient of the inertia group, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I^+_{{\textsf{k}}}$$\end{document}Ik+ is the wild inertia subgroup. The depth-zero condition means that the parameter \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda $$\end{document}λ in the introduction factors through \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I_t$$\end{document}It. Suppose \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G^\vee $$\end{document}G∨ is semisimple. a Weyl group element \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w\in W^\vee =N_{G^\vee }(T^\vee )/T^\vee $$\end{document}w∈W∨=NG∨(T∨)/T∨ such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w^\vee \circ \theta ^\vee \circ \lambda ^q=\lambda $$\end{document}w∨∘θ∨∘λq=λ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${T^\vee }^{w^\vee \theta ^\vee }$$\end{document}T∨w∨θ∨ is finite.

Proposition 3.3

The geometric wavefront set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${}^{{\bar{{{\textsf{k}}}}}}\textsf{WF}(\pi (\lambda ,w,0,x))$$\end{document}k¯WF(π(λ,w,0,x)) is the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\textbf{G}}}({\bar{{{\textsf{k}}}}})$$\end{document}G(k¯)-saturation of the principal nilpotent orbit of the maximal pseudo-Levi subgroup \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Z_{{{\textbf{G}}}({\bar{{{\textsf{k}}}}})}(x).$$\end{document}ZG(k¯)(x).

Proof

The claim follows at once, after we recall the construction of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi (\lambda ,w,0,x)$$\end{document}π(λ,w,0,x). The element x defines a cocycle \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_x\in H^1({{\textsf{k}}}, {{\textbf{G}}})$$\end{document}ux∈H1(k,G) which gives the inner twist \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega _x$$\end{document}ωx of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\textbf{G}}}$$\end{document}G and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F_x$$\end{document}Fx a twisted Frobenius map and a maximal parahoric subgroup \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\textbf{G}}}_x$$\end{document}Gx. Then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\textbf{G}}}_x^{F_x}$$\end{document}Gxx is a maximal parahoric subgroup of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\textbf{G}}}^{\omega _x}({{\textsf{k}}})$$\end{document}Gωx(k). The pair (w, x) defines an \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F_x$$\end{document}Fx-minisotropic torus \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_x$$\end{document}Tx and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda $$\end{document}λ defines a depth-zero character \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi _x$$\end{document}χx of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_x$$\end{document}Tx. Then Deligne–Lusztig induction defines an irreducible cuspidal representation of the reductive quotient \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{{{\textbf{G}}}_x^{F_x}}$$\end{document}Gxx¯\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \kappa _x=\pm R_{T_x}^{\overline{{{\textbf{G}}}_x^{F_x}}}(\chi _x). $$\end{document}κx=±RTxGxx¯(χx).The important thing for us is that this cuspidal representation is generic, in the sense that the Kawanaka wavefront set is principal. The supercuspidal representation is compactly induced\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \pi (\lambda ,w,0,x)=\operatorname {ind}_{{\textbf{Z}}^{F_x}{{\textbf{G}}}_x^{F_x}}^{{{\textbf{G}}}^{\omega _x}({{\textsf{k}}})}({{\widetilde{\kappa }}}_x). $$\end{document}π(λ,w,0,x)=indZFxGxxGωx(k)(κ~x).The wavefront set is given by Theorem 3.1. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Conjecture 1.6 clearly holds. Moreover, so does Conjecture 1.9(2) since the L-packet of supercuspidals contains a generic supercuspidal representation.

Depth-zero supercuspidal representations of classical groups

In [24], Lust and Stevens constructed L-packets of depth-zero supercuspidal representations of classical groups. We will verify Conjecture 1.6 for these L-packets. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {G}$$\end{document}G be a connected reductive group of classical type over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\mathbb {F}}}_q$$\end{document}Fq, q odd. Let F be the Frobenius map. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {G}^*$$\end{document}G∗ be the dual group over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\mathbb {F}}}_q$$\end{document}Fq with dual Frobenius also denoted F. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsf{Irr}\, \mathcal {G}^F$$\end{document}IrrGF be the set of equivalence classes of irreducible complex \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {G}^F$$\end{document}GF-representations. According to Lusztig’s classification, we can partition\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \textsf{Irr}\, \mathcal {G}^F=\sqcup _s \textsf{Irr}_s \mathcal {G}^F, $$\end{document}IrrGF=⊔sIrrsGF,where s runs over the conjugacy classes of semisimple elements in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {G}^*}^F$$\end{document}G∗F. The piece \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsf{Irr}_1 \mathcal {G}^F$$\end{document}Irr1GF is the set of irreducible unipotent representations. Since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Z(\mathcal {G})$$\end{document}Z(G) is connected, the rational semisimple conjugacy classes in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {G}^*$$\end{document}G∗ are in one-to-one correspondence with the geometric F-stable semisimple conjugacy classes in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {G}^*$$\end{document}G∗ [5, Proposition 3.7.3].

The above decomposition is realized as follows. By [5, Theorem 4.4.6], there exists a natural one-to-one correspondence between geometric conjugacy classes of pairs \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(T,\theta )$$\end{document}(T,θ), where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {T}$$\end{document}T is a maximal F-stable torus in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {G}$$\end{document}G and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta $$\end{document}θ is a character of T, and F-stable semisimple conjugacy classes in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {G}^*$$\end{document}G∗. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s\in \mathcal {T}^*\subset \mathcal {G}^*$$\end{document}s∈T∗⊂G∗, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textsf{Irr}_s \mathcal {G}^F$$\end{document}IrrsGF consists of all the constituents of the induced representations \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_{\mathcal {T}}^{\mathcal {G}}(s)$$\end{document}RTG(s) where we think of s as a character of T.

Lusztig’s Jordan decomposition says that there is a one-to-one correspondence\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \Psi _s^{\mathcal {G}}:\textsf{Irr}_s \mathcal {G}^F\longleftrightarrow \textsf{Irr}_1(\mathcal {G}_s^F), $$\end{document}ΨsG:IrrsGF⟷Irr1(GsF),where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {G}_s$$\end{document}Gs is the endoscopic group of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {G}$$\end{document}G with dual \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {G}^*_s:=Z_{\mathcal {G}^*}(s)$$\end{document}Gs∗:=ZG∗(s). To have cuspidal representations, we need \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Z(\mathcal {G}^*)^0$$\end{document}Z(G∗)0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Z(\mathcal {G}_s^*)^0$$\end{document}Z(Gs∗)0 to have the same F-rank. In other words, the set of cuspidal \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {G}^F$$\end{document}GF-representations are in one-to-one correspondence with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {G}^*$$\end{document}G∗-pairs \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(s,\tau )$$\end{document}(s,τ) where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s\in {\mathcal {G}^*}^F$$\end{document}s∈G∗F is semisimple of equal rank and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau $$\end{document}τ is a cuspidal unipotent representation of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {G}_s^F$$\end{document}GsF.

if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {G}=Sp(2n)$$\end{document}G=Sp(2n), then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_+=2(m_+^2+m_+)+1$$\end{document}a+=2(m+2+m+)+1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_-=2m_-^2$$\end{document}a-=2m-2.

Example 4.1

A cuspidal representation of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} Nilpotent Langlands parameters

Let G be a classical group over a p-adic field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\textsf{k}}}$$\end{document}k. Every depth-zero supercuspidal representation is compactly-induced4.2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \pi =\text {ind}_K^G(\tau _1\boxtimes \tau _2), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}π=indKG(τ1⊠τ2),where K is the normalizer of a maximal parahoric subgroup with reductive quotient \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\bar{K}}={\mathcal {G}}^\circ _{n_1,n_2}$$\end{document}K¯=Gn1,n2∘ over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\mathbb {F}}}_q$$\end{document}Fq, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau _1\boxtimes \tau _2$$\end{document}τ1⊠τ2 is a cuspidal representation of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {G}}^\circ _{n_1,n_2}({{\mathbb {F}}}_q)$$\end{document}Gn1,n2∘(Fq). Lust and Stevens [24, §1 and §8] attach to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi $$\end{document}π the nilpotent orbit \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\mathbb {O}}}^\vee _\pi $$\end{document}Oπ∨ given by the partition obtained by the following procedure. For every integer \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} Partitions

We record several elementary facts about partitions that will be used in the proofs in the next subsections.

Definition 4.2

If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha ,\beta $$\end{document}α,β are two partitions of m, n, respectively, the sum \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha +\beta $$\end{document}α+β is a partition of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m+n$$\end{document}m+n given as follows. Suppose \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha =(\alpha _1,\alpha _2,\ldots ,\alpha _\ell )$$\end{document}α=(α1,α2,…,αℓ) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta =(\beta _1,\beta _2,\ldots ,\beta _\ell )$$\end{document}β=(β1,β2,…,βℓ) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha _1\ge \alpha _2\ge \cdots \ge \alpha _\ell \ge 0$$\end{document}α1≥α2≥⋯≥αℓ≥0, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta _1\ge \beta _2\ge \cdots \ge \beta _\ell \ge 0$$\end{document}β1≥β2≥⋯≥βℓ≥0. (By allowing entries of 0, we may assume there is the same number of parts in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha $$\end{document}α and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta $$\end{document}β.) Then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha +\beta =(\alpha _1+\beta _1,\alpha _2+\beta _2,\ldots ,\alpha _\ell +\beta _\ell ).$$\end{document}α+β=(α1+β1,α2+β2,…,αℓ+βℓ).

The above operation on partitions plays a key role in the explicit computation of j-induction for representations of the Weyl group of classical types.

If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha $$\end{document}α and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta $$\end{document}β are two partitions, denote by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \sqcup \beta $$\end{document}α⊔β the partition obtained by concatenation (and reordering). If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha $$\end{document}α is a partition, let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha ^t$$\end{document}αt denote the transpose partition. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \le \beta $$\end{document}α≤β denote the dominance order of partitions.

any four partitions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \le \beta $$\end{document}α≤β then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha ^t\ge \beta ^t$$\end{document}αt≥βt.

If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \le \beta $$\end{document}α≤β then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha ^t\ge \beta ^t$$\end{document}αt≥βt.

If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha _1\le \beta _1$$\end{document}α1≤β1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha _2\le \beta _2$$\end{document}α2≤β2 then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha _1\sqcup \alpha _2\le \beta _1\sqcup \beta _2$$\end{document}α1⊔α2≤β1⊔β2.

If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha _1\le \beta _1$$\end{document}α1≤β1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha _2\le \beta _2$$\end{document}α2≤β2 then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha _1\sqcup \alpha _2\le \beta _1\sqcup \beta _2$$\end{document}α1⊔α2≤β1⊔β2.

For any two partitions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha ,\beta $$\end{document}α,β, 4.3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} (\alpha \sqcup \beta )^t=\alpha ^t+\beta ^t \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(α⊔β)t=αt+βt

For any two partitions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha ,\beta $$\end{document}α,β, 4.3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} (\alpha \sqcup \beta )^t=\alpha ^t+\beta ^t \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(α⊔β)t=αt+βt

For any four partitions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha _1,\alpha _2,\beta _1,\beta _2$$\end{document}α1,α2,β1,β2, 4.4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} (\alpha _1\sqcup \beta _1)+(\alpha _2\sqcup \beta _2)\ge (\alpha _1+\alpha _2)\sqcup (\beta _1+\beta _2). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(α1⊔β1)+(α2⊔β2)≥(α1+α2)⊔(β1+β2).

For any four partitions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha _1,\alpha _2,\beta _1,\beta _2$$\end{document}α1,α2,β1,β2, 4.4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} (\alpha _1\sqcup \beta _1)+(\alpha _2\sqcup \beta _2)\ge (\alpha _1+\alpha _2)\sqcup (\beta _1+\beta _2). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(α1⊔β1)+(α2⊔β2)≥(α1+α2)⊔(β1+β2).

For any three partitions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha ,\beta ,\gamma $$\end{document}α,β,γ, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \ge \beta $$\end{document}α≥β, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha +\gamma \ge \beta +\gamma $$\end{document}α+γ≥β+γ.

For any three partitions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha ,\beta ,\gamma $$\end{document}α,β,γ, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \ge \beta $$\end{document}α≥β, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha +\gamma \ge \beta +\gamma $$\end{document}α+γ≥β+γ.

Proof

Claims (1), (2), (3) are elementary and well known, see [8, §5.1] for example. Claim (5) follows from (2) and (3). We prove (4). Let us first prove the case of three partitions:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \beta _1+(\alpha \sqcup \beta _2)\ge \alpha \sqcup (\beta _1+\beta _2). $$\end{document}β1+(α⊔β2)≥α⊔(β1+β2).by induction on the length of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha $$\end{document}α. If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha =\emptyset $$\end{document}α=∅, this is clearly true. Suppose \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha =\{s\}$$\end{document}α={s} is a singleton. Write \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta _1=(a_1\ge a_2\ge \cdots \ge a_k)$$\end{document}β1=(a1≥a2≥⋯≥ak) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta _2=(b_1\ge b_2\ge \cdots \ge b_k)$$\end{document}β2=(b1≥b2≥⋯≥bk) and suppose i and t are the smallest indices such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b_i>s\ge b_{i+1}$$\end{document}bi>s≥bi+1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_t+b_t>s\ge a_{t+1}+b_{t+1}$$\end{document}at+bt>s≥at+1+bt+1. (We think \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_0=b_0=\infty $$\end{document}a0=b0=∞ for the consistency of the notation.) Then\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \beta _1+(\beta _2\sqcup \{s\}):\ a_1+b_1\ge a_2+b_2\ge & \cdots \ge a_i+b_i\ge a_{i+1}+s\ge a_{i+2}+b_{i+1}\\\ge & \cdots \ge a_k+b_{k-1}\ge b_k, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}β1+(β2⊔{s}):a1+b1≥a2+b2≥⋯≥ai+bi≥ai+1+s≥ai+2+bi+1≥⋯≥ak+bk-1≥bk,while\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ (\beta _1+\beta _2)\sqcup \{s\}:\ a_1+b_1\ge a_2+b_2\ge \cdots \ge a_t+b_t> s\ge a_{t+1}+b_{t+1}\ge \cdots \ge a_{k}+b_k. $$\end{document}(β1+β2)⊔{s}:a1+b1≥a2+b2≥⋯≥at+bt>s≥at+1+bt+1≥⋯≥ak+bk.and it is immediate that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta _1+(\beta _2\sqcup \{s\})\ge (\beta _1+\beta _2)\sqcup \{s\}$$\end{document}β1+(β2⊔{s})≥(β1+β2)⊔{s}.

Now suppose the inequality holds for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha $$\end{document}α and consider \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\widetilde{\alpha }}=\alpha \sqcup \{s\}$$\end{document}α~=α⊔{s}. Denote \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\widetilde{\beta }}_2=\beta _2\sqcup \{s\}$$\end{document}β~2=β2⊔{s}. Then\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \beta _1+({\widetilde{\alpha }}\sqcup \beta _2)=\beta _1+(\alpha \sqcup {\widetilde{\beta }}_2)\ge \alpha \sqcup (\beta _1+{\widetilde{\beta }}_2)\ge \alpha \sqcup \{s\}\sqcup (\beta _1+\beta _2)={\widetilde{\alpha }}\sqcup (\beta _1+\beta _2), $$\end{document}β1+(α~⊔β2)=β1+(α⊔β~2)≥α⊔(β1+β~2)≥α⊔{s}⊔(β1+β2)=α~⊔(β1+β2),where the first inequality follows by the inductive step and the second by the case of a singleton. This proves the case of three partitions.

The inequality for four partitions (4.4) follows by induction on the length of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha _1$$\end{document}α1. If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha _1=\emptyset $$\end{document}α1=∅, this is the case of three partitions. Suppose \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha _1=(a_1\ge a_2\ge \cdots \ge a_k>0)$$\end{document}α1=(a1≥a2≥⋯≥ak>0) is a partition and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\bar{\alpha }}_1=(a_1\ge a_2\ge \cdots \ge a_{k-1})$$\end{document}α¯1=(a1≥a2≥⋯≥ak-1) so that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha _1={\bar{\alpha }}_1\sqcup \{a_k\}$$\end{document}α1=α¯1⊔{ak}. On the right hand side \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_k$$\end{document}ak is added to the k-th part \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_k'$$\end{document}ak′ of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha _2$$\end{document}α2 (written in decreasing order). In the left hand side, if we write \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha _2\sqcup \beta _2=(c_1\ge c_2\ge \cdots )$$\end{document}α2⊔β2=(c1≥c2≥⋯), then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a_k$$\end{document}ak is added to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c_j$$\end{document}cj \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$j\ge k$$\end{document}j≥k, such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c_i\ge a_k'$$\end{document}ci≥ak′ for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i\le j$$\end{document}i≤j. Lemma 4.4

The irreducible \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W_n$$\end{document}Wn-representation which is j-induced\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} j_{\prod _P S_{a_P}^{k_P}}^{W_n}\left( \boxtimes _P (m_P,\ldots ,2,1)^{\boxtimes k_P}\right) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}j∏PSaPPn⊠P(mP,…,2,1)⊠kPis attached in the Springer correspondence to the symplectic nilpotent orbit\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \sum _P (2m_Pk_P,\ldots ,4k_P,2k_P), $$\end{document}∑P(2mPkP,…,4kP,2kP),where the sum is in the sense of Definition 4.2.

Proof

By the calculation leading up to (4.6), we need to show that the nilpotent orbit for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$j_ {\prod _P W_{k_Pa_P}}^{W_n}(\boxtimes _P\sigma _P)$$\end{document}j∏PWkPaPn(⊠PσP) is as claimed in the statement of the lemma. Since j-induction works in stages, this comes down to the case of two polynomials \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$P_1,P_2$$\end{document}P1,P2, that is to the easy verification that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$j_{W_1\times W_2}^{W}(\sigma _{1}\boxtimes \sigma _{2})$$\end{document}jW1×W2W(σ1⊠σ2) is the Springer representation for the nilpotent \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(2m_1k_1,\ldots ,4k_1,2k_1)+(2m_2k_2,\ldots ,4k_2,2k_2)$$\end{document}(2m1k1,…,4k1,2k1)+(2m2k2,…,4k2,2k2). \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Conjecture 1.6 holds in this case.

Proof

Claim (1) is just a restatement of the Lust–Stevens parametrization. Notice the presence of the entry [1] because of the step (2 s) in the procedure. Claim (2) is directly implied by Lemma 4.4. It remains to verify (3).

Suppose first that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_1=n$$\end{document}n1=n, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_2=0$$\end{document}n2=0, this is the hyperspecial case. Write \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m_P$$\end{document}mP for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m_P^{(1)}$$\end{document}mP(1). Then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb {O}}^\vee _\pi $$\end{document}Oπ∨ is simply\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ {{\mathbb {O}}}^\vee _\pi =[1]\sqcup \bigsqcup _P (\underbrace{m_P,\ldots ,m_P}_{2k_P},\underbrace{m_P-1,\ldots ,m_P-1}_{2k_P},\ldots ,\underbrace{1,\ldots ,1}_{2k_P}), $$\end{document}Oπ∨=[1]⊔⨆P(mP,…,mP⏟2kP,mP-1,…,mP-1⏟2kP,…,1,…,1⏟2kP),while\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} Moreover\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} (\underbrace{m_P,\ldots ,m_P}_{2k_P},\underbrace{m_P-1,\ldots ,m_P-1}_{2k_P},\ldots ,\underbrace{1,\ldots ,1}_{2k_P})^t =(2m_P k_p,\ldots ,4k_P,2k_P)\end{aligned}$$\end{document}(mP,…,mP⏟2kP,mP-1,…,mP-1⏟2kP,…,1,…,1⏟2kP)t=(2mPkp,…,4kP,2kP)which proves that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d^\vee ({{\mathbb {O}}}_\pi ^\vee )={}^{\bar{{\textsf{k}}}}\textsf{WF}(\pi )$$\end{document}d∨(Oπ∨)=k¯WF(π) if there is a single P. If there is more than one polynomial P, the claim follows as well by the compatibility (4.3) for two (or more) partitions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha ,\beta $$\end{document}α,β.

Now suppose both \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_1,n_2>0$$\end{document}n1,n2>0. The key observation is that for all k and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m^{(1)}\ge m^{(2)}$$\end{document}m(1)≥m(2),\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&d^\vee ([1]\sqcup [m^{(1)}+m^{(2)}]^{2k}\sqcup [|m^{(1)}-m^{(2)}|-1]^{2k})\\&\quad =\left( (m^{(1)}+m^{(2)},m^{(1)}+m^{(2)}-2,\ldots ,m^{(1)}-m^{(2)}+2\right. ,\\&\qquad \left. m^{(1)}-m^{(2)},m^{(1)}-m^{(2)}-1,\ldots ,2,1)^{2k}\right) ^t\\&\quad =(2m^{(1)}k,\ldots ,4k,2k)\sqcup (2m^{(2)}k,\ldots ,4k,2k), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}d∨([1]⊔[m(1)+m(2)]2k⊔[|m(1)-m(2)|-1]2k)=(m(1)+m(2),m(1)+m(2)-2,…,m(1)-m(2)+2,m(1)-m(2),m(1)-m(2)-1,…,2,1)2kt=(2m(1)k,…,4k,2k)⊔(2m(2)k,…,4k,2k),which proves (3) (with equality in Conjecture 1.6) in the case of a single polynomial P.

Finally, in the general case with more polynomials, we use again the compatibility (4.3) and see that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} d^\vee ({{\mathbb {O}}}^\vee _\pi )&=\left( \bigsqcup _P [m_P^{(1)}+m_P^{(2)}]^{2k_P}\sqcup [|m_P^{(1)}-m_P^{(2)}|-1]^{2k_P}\right) ^t\\&=\sum _P \left( (2m_P^{(1)} k_P,\ldots ,4k_P,2k_P)\sqcup (2m_P^{(2)} k_P,\ldots ,4k_P,2k_P)\right) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}d∨(Oπ∨)=⨆P[mP(1)+mP(2)]2kP⊔[|mP(1)-mP(2)|-1]2kPt=∑P(2mP(1)kP,…,4kP,2kP)⊔(2mP(2)kP,…,4kP,2kP)Using Lemma 4.3(5) and applying the inequality (4.4) repeatedly, one can see that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d^\vee ({{\mathbb {O}}}^\vee _\pi )$$\end{document}d∨(Oπ∨) is greater in the partition order than \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${}^{\bar{{\textsf{k}}}}\textsf{WF}(\pi )=\bigsqcup _{i=1,2}\Big (\sum _P(2m_P^{(i)} k_p,\ldots ,4k_P,2k_P)\Big )$$\end{document}k¯WF(π)=⨆i=1,2(∑P(2mP(i)kp,…,4kP,2kP)). Notice that the difference between the two formulae is simply that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Sigma $$\end{document}Σ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sqcup _{i=1,2}$$\end{document}⊔i=1,2 are interchanged.

To see that the inequality \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${}^{\bar{{\textsf{k}}}}\textsf{WF}(\pi )\le d^\vee ({{\mathbb {O}}}^\vee _\pi )$$\end{document}k¯WF(π)≤d∨(Oπ∨) can be strict, consider the case when there are just two distinct polynomials \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$P_1, P_2$$\end{document}P1,P2, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$P_i$$\end{document}Pi occurs on the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Sp(2n_i)$$\end{document}Sp(2ni) factor. Then\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ ^{\bar{{\textsf{k}}}}\textsf{WF}(\pi )=(2m_{P_1}^{(1)} k_{P_1},\ldots ,4k_{P_1},2k_{P_1})\sqcup (2m_{P_2}^{(2)} k_{P_2},\ldots ,4k_{P_2},2k_{P_2}) $$\end{document}k¯WF(π)=(2mP1(1)kP1,…,4kP1,2kP1)⊔(2mP2(2)kP2,…,4kP2,2kP2)while\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ d^\vee ({{\mathbb {O}}}^\vee _\pi )=(2m_{P_1}^{(1)} k_{P_1},\ldots ,4k_{P_1},2k_{P_1})+(2m_{P_2}^{(2)} k_{P_2},\ldots ,4k_{P_2},2k_{P_2}). $$\end{document}d∨(Oπ∨)=(2mP1(1)kP1,…,4kP1,2kP1)+(2mP2(2)kP2,…,4kP2,2kP2).\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

The other extreme is that only endoscopic factors of type B and D occur in the construction of the cuspidal representation on the finite reductive group of the parahoric \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Sp(2n_1)\times Sp(2n_2)$$\end{document}Sp(2n1)×Sp(2n2).

Proposition 4.6

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi $$\end{document}π be a depth-zero supercuspidal \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Sp(2n,{\textsf{k}})$$\end{document}Sp(2n,k)-representation as above. Suppose there are no endoscopic unitary factors, i.e., no polynomials P with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\deg P>1$$\end{document}degP>1 appear. Then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\mathbb {O}}}^\vee _\pi =[2(m_+^{(1)}+m_+^{(2)})+1]\sqcup [2|m_+^{(1)}-m_+^{(2)}|-1]\sqcup [2(m_-^{(1)}+m_-^{(2)})-1]\sqcup [2|m_-^{(1)}-m_-^{(2)}|-1].$$\end{document}Oπ∨=[2(m+(1)+m+(2))+1]⊔[2|m+(1)-m+(2)|-1]⊔[2(m-(1)+m-(2))-1]⊔[2|m-(1)-m-(2)|-1].Conjecture 1.6 holds in this case.

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\mathbb {O}}}^\vee _\pi =[2(m_+^{(1)}+m_+^{(2)})+1]\sqcup [2|m_+^{(1)}-m_+^{(2)}|-1]\sqcup [2(m_-^{(1)}+m_-^{(2)})-1]\sqcup [2|m_-^{(1)}-m_-^{(2)}|-1].$$\end{document}Oπ∨=[2(m+(1)+m+(2))+1]⊔[2|m+(1)-m+(2)|-1]⊔[2(m-(1)+m-(2))-1]⊔[2|m-(1)-m-(2)|-1].

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\mathbb {O}}}^\vee _\pi =[2(m_+^{(1)}+m_+^{(2)})+1]\sqcup [2|m_+^{(1)}-m_+^{(2)}|-1]\sqcup [2(m_-^{(1)}+m_-^{(2)})-1]\sqcup [2|m_-^{(1)}-m_-^{(2)}|-1].$$\end{document}Oπ∨=[2(m+(1)+m+(2))+1]⊔[2|m+(1)-m+(2)|-1]⊔[2(m-(1)+m-(2))-1]⊔[2|m-(1)-m-(2)|-1].

Conjecture 1.6 holds in this case.

Conjecture 1.6 holds in this case.

Proof

Claim (1) is just a restatement of the above procedure by Lust–Stevens, listed here for convenience. Claim (2) follows from formula (3.1) by a direct calculation using j-induction. More precisely, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${}^{\bar{{\textsf{k}}}}\textsf{WF}(\pi )$$\end{document}k¯WF(π) is the saturation (concatenation in terms of the partitions) of the two Kawanaka wavefront set nilpotent orbits on the two factors \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Sp(2n_i)$$\end{document}Sp(2ni) of the maximal parahoric.

Fix one factor and drop the index (i) from the notation for simplicity. The Kawanaka wavefront set is the symplectic nilpotent orbit for which the Springer representation (attached to the trivial local system) is given the j-induced\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} j_{W(B)\times {W(D)}}^{W_n} & \left( (m_+,\ldots ,2,1)\times (m_+,\ldots ,2,1))\right. \\ & \left. \boxtimes (m_-,\ldots ,2,1)\times (m_--1,\ldots ,2,1))\right) , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}jW(B)×W(D)n(m+,…,2,1)×(m+,…,2,1))⊠(m-,…,2,1)×(m--1,…,2,1)),where the notation indicates that the first factor is of type B and the second of type D. We use the customary bipartition notation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \times \beta $$\end{document}α×β for an irreducible representation of the Weyl groups of types B or D.

Using the formulae for j-induction from [27, §5], we see this j-induced representation is the bipartition\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \left( (m_+,\ldots ,2,1)+(m_-,\ldots ,2,1)\right) \times \left( (m_+,\ldots ,2,1)+(m_--1,\ldots ,2,1)\right) , $$\end{document}(m+,…,2,1)+(m-,…,2,1)×(m+,…,2,1)+(m--1,…,2,1),where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$+$$\end{document}+ has the meaning from Definition 4.2. Applying the combinatorial algorithm for the Springer representation for type C, we find that the corresponding symplectic nilpotent orbit is\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \,[2(m_++m_-)]\sqcup {\left\{ \begin{array}{ll},& m_+\ge m_-,\\ \,[2(m_--m_+-1)],& m_+< m_- \end{array}\right. }, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}[2(m++m-)]⊔,m+≥m-,[2(m--m+-1)],m+<m-,and Claim (2) is proven.

Claim (3) follows by computing Spaltenstein duality \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d^\vee ({{\mathbb {O}}}^\vee _\pi )$$\end{document}d∨(Oπ∨) using formulas (4.7) and (4.3). We begin with the hyperspecial case: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_2=0$$\end{document}n2=0, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m_+^{(2)}=m_-^{(2)}=0$$\end{document}m+(2)=m-(2)=0, and explain how we get an equality \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d^\vee ({{\mathbb {O}}}^\vee _\pi )={}^{\bar{{\textsf{k}}}}\textsf{WF}(\pi )$$\end{document}d∨(Oπ∨)=k¯WF(π).

For simplicity, denote \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a=m_+^{(1)}$$\end{document}a=m+(1), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b=m_-^{(1)}$$\end{document}b=m-(1). Then the partition for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb {O}}_\pi ^\vee $$\end{document}Oπ∨ is\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\underline{p}}=[2a+1]\sqcup [2a-1]\sqcup [2b-1]\sqcup [2b-1]. $$\end{document}p_=[2a+1]⊔[2a-1]⊔[2b-1]⊔[2b-1].Firstly, notice that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ (2a+1,2a-1,2a-3,\ldots ,3,1)^t=(a+1,a,a,a-1,a-1,\ldots ,2,2,1,1), $$\end{document}(2a+1,2a-1,2a-3,…,3,1)t=(a+1,a,a,a-1,a-1,…,2,2,1,1),an identity that we will use repeatedly in the proof. Notice that one could be tempted to use the inequality\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ ((\gamma _+^M+\gamma _-^M)\sqcup (\gamma _+^m+\gamma _-^m))^{-}_C\ge (\gamma _+^M+\gamma _-^M)^{-}_C\sqcup (\gamma _+^m+\gamma _-^m), $$\end{document}((γ+M+γ-M)⊔(γ+m+γ-m))C-≥(γ+M+γ-M)C-⊔(γ+m+γ-m),noting that in this case \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\gamma _+^m+\gamma _-^m)$$\end{document}(γ+m+γ-m) is already a partition of type C. Moreover, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\gamma _+^M+\gamma _-^M)^{-}_C$$\end{document}(γ+M+γ-M)C- equals, by (4.8), exactly the union of two partitions in the wavefront set of the first factor (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i=1$$\end{document}i=1). But \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma _+^m+\gamma _-^m$$\end{document}γ+m+γ-m is smaller than the union of the two partitions in the wavefront set of the second factor (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i=2$$\end{document}i=2). So the C-collapse can’t be separated in this computation.

This completes the proof.

Theorem 4.9

Conjecture 1.6 holds in the case when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi $$\end{document}π be a depth-zero supercuspidal \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Sp(2n,{\textsf{k}})$$\end{document}Sp(2n,k)-representation.

Unramified unitary groups

In this case, the calculation is simply that from Proposition 4.5, so there is nothing more to check.

Special orthogonal groups

The calculation is similar to that for the symplectic group. The part of the calculation pertaining to the polynomials P of degree strictly greater than 1 is identical to Proposition 4.5. Thus, we only need to prove the analogue of Proposition 4.6 for orthogonal groups.

Ramified unitary groups

For the ramified unitary groups, the finite group \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {G}}^\circ _{n_1,n_2}({{\mathbb {F}}}_q)$$\end{document}Gn1,n2∘(Fq) on which the cuspidal representation lives is a product of a symplectic and special orthogonal group. We only record the analogue of Propositions 4.6 and 4.10. The proof is simply a combination of the symplectic and orthogonal cases discussed before.

Tempered L-packets of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{{\textbf{G}}}_2}$$\end{document}G2

The verification of the main conjectures for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G_2$$\end{document}G2
